Book VII
2042

Collections of receipts for preserves
cordials, medicine and cookery
[c. 1700]





Thes are the Dukes of the sons of Esau
Eliphas the first born son of Esau Duke
teman





Lady Betty Southalls Elix
er a gainst the Cholicx

with MS 4054


Take of Leaves of Senna, three ounces
of Liquirish, anniseeds, coriander seeds,
Elecampane root dry'd and sliced,
Guiacum, of each two ounces.
Raisins wasth and stoned, one pound.
Steep these in three quarts of the
best Aqua Vitee, or Brandy, A
fortnight, or till the Liquor become
of a redish tincture. the powr it
off, and keep it in glass Bottles.

Take 4 or 5 spoonfulls in any colicke
sickness of the stomach, or fitt of the
stone in the kidneys, or any indigestion
of the stomach, it is good for all kinds
of Gripes whatsoever.
Repeat the like dose after 5 or 6 hours if





if there be need.





To Make Cherry Wine

To 4 gallons of water 24 pounds of Malingoe reasons pickt
from the sticks & shred small let them lye 3 days in steep
in spring water & stir it twice a day then strain it throu=
gh a hair seiue & boyle the liquor half an houer & when
it is cold put to yr reasons 3 gallons of e Juce of Cherry
& put it into a 7 gallon uessill & let stand 3

1.
Receits for Preserving

To make Jelly of Pepins
Cutt the greenest Pepins you can gett into quarters
then putt them ito a Skelet with as much water as
will cover them; So boyle them quick till the taste
of pepins be pretty well out, the Syrup run through
a strainer and take to every all pinte of the Syrup
a pound of double refine Sugar,then sett it over
a Very quick fire and lett it boyle as fast as may be
till it will Jelly; then putt in good Store of Jüice of
lemons and Oranges a raw lemons pill cutt about of
bigness of brown thred and not above an inch long
it must not boyle after the iüie of lemons be in; So
putt it up in glasses.

To make Marmelade of quinces
Take Yellow quinces, pare & quarter them, and to
a pound of quinces take a pound of Sugar white and
finely beaten, then take half the sugar & putt it into
a Sylver Bason with your quinces , and sett them on the
fire, and when the Sugar is melted, lett it boyle as fast
as you can, and as it boyle putt in more of the Sugar
till it is all putt in ; and when yout Marmelade begins
to look clear putt in half a pint of liquor of quinces
& so lett it boyle 2 or 3 warmes, then skim it & putt it up,
the liquor of quicnces must be warmed before it be putt
into the Marmelade. Some putt but half a pod of Sugar to a  Pod of quinces &
noe liquor at all.
To make Liquor of quinces
Take some quinces & wipe them cleane & quarter them, have a Skellet
of fatte ready on the fire & putt in tow pieces att a time; when they
be somting tender take them out, and outt then into a piece of
tifinis and ring the juice clear out between two trenchors & soe
putt in more till you have the quantity of Juice you ought to have
the quinces must be hott when to putt them into the tiffinis
els they will get noe juice.





To make Cleare Cakes of White Quinces.

Take your quinces afsoon as they be ripe, pare them
coare them & quarter them; then have ready a litle
of cunditt water on the fire, and when it boyles putt
in the quinces and lett them boyle in it till they begin
to be tender, (but not soft att all lest they palp) then take
them out; and as hott as you can wring them thorough
a strong linnen Cloth into a glasse, (and take heed the
quinces palp come not through with the juice) you may putt
a spoonfull or two of the liquor in the straining of them
(butt the less the better & whiter it will be) then weigh this
Juice allowing for the weigh of the glass and take full wheigh
of the iuice and the third putt over and above of double
refined sugar; make your syrup with a litle water as
will well wett the sugar and boile it to a Candy hatch
then powr in your Juice and stirr it together and lett it
be throwghly hott mingled, but lett it not boyle; then take
it of the fire and putt it into sawcers & plates and
when it is cold sett them in a stove and with a temperate
fire dry them and when they are half candied        the
topp cutt them round and turne them out vpon plates, and
putt them again to the stove.

An Excellent way to make either read or white
Quinces Mamelade

Take a poun of quinces after it is pared and cutt it in
pretty thin slices not to thin and a pound of loafe sugar
beaten very fine, then take halfe a pinte of the Juice of
grated quinces and wash you skellet with faire water, (for -
that will make it not burn) so then putt in your things -
and cover it close with a plate tied in a cloth, sett it on the
fire and now and then shake ithe skellet to keep it from -
burning, and then you think it is of a pure colour uncover it
and breake your quinces as much as you please & boyle it
a pace to marmelade; If you scald your quinces it will be
sooner done, If you make it afsoone as you gather your quinces
it

3
Preserving

it will be better colored and sooner marmelade, for they -
will be tender; then when they boyle it you will have it
white, boyle it as fast as you can uncovred and doble refined
sugar, and lett not your grated quinces lye to long before
you straine it, but strow your sugar over it afsoone as you can.

To make Dutch wasles.

Take 2lb of the best flower & 13 Eggs half the white of
them; one quarte of Creame and a pinte of milk; a quarter
of a pound of naples Biskets, or else halfe a dozen of Dutch
Biskets beaten very small; a pound of melted butter a spoonfull
or two of ale yest & must stand 2 or 3 hours to raise, and
when you serve them you must putt some melted butter &
sugar upon them.

Almonds Ginger Bread.

One pound of almonds. a pound & a quarter of sugar.
2 grains of ambregris. one of musk. 4 ounces of Cinamon.
a good spoonfull of ginger. as much of nuttmegs as will
lye on a sheling. as much cloves and mace as will lye on
a six pence; the sugar and spices must be finely searched
the almonds finely beaten with rose water to keepe them
from oyling, so putt the spices to the almonds into a
marble mortar, beate it to a paste mixing with it about
one third of the sugar but keep out the other two third
of the sugar to mould it up in and so print it of in litle
thin cakes.
The white is the same of the read only leaving out the Ci-
-namon and observing for the white the almonds must not
be mixed in the mortar (least it should goe into palp for
want of the Cinamon to dry it, but must be rolld up into -
paste with the sugar and other spices above mentioned.
Take the best Jordans Almonds & beate them very fine with
some rose water, then you must have doble refin'd sugar, beat it
very fine and search it with a fine cive, then take ginger, beat &
search it very fine; that which must be white must have nothing
but ginger and sugar; and that which is read must be done with
Cinamon & searched a litle ginger amongst it to give it a taste, it
must mix like a paste rolls out thin and laid upon paper & sett it before
the fire to dry, any oven will spoile it, for it will run about.

28





4
Preserving
To make a Seed cake
Take eight pounds of flower and three pounds of Butter
three pints of new milk, a quarter of a pinte of rose water
and a full pinte of good Ale yest; heate the milk and butter
in it, then coole it a little that it scald not the yest, then
putt the flower and milk together and the Yest, and lett it
Lye and rise halfe and houre , then three pounds of white
sugar and half a pound of Garroway seed, mingle them
with the sugar, putt them into the cake stirring it till
you putt it into the case maid of, it must stand one
houre and a quarter in the oven. Half of this proportion
will make a good Cake, and if you make but half of this
proportion then it must stand but three quarter of an houre
in the oven.

To preserve Goosberries Very green
Stone a pound of green Goosberries and putt them in
cold water, as you doe them, lett them stand over a soft fire
till they will peele close coured, them take them of e fire, peele
them and putt them into the same water, left them stand
over a Soft fire a greening, Boile a pound of double refined
Sugar with as much water as will well boyle it, Skim it and
putt it to the Goosberries, lett them lye a scalding till they
be Very green upon a soft fire, cover them all e time
when they be greened enough, take them up, and lett the Syrop
boyle a little longer, then putt them up.

To dry mussell Plums
Take them and stone them, putt them in an earthen pott a layer
of plums and a layer of sugar, according to your discretion till your
pott is full. Then lye them downe with a paper, and bake them tender
then take them one by one and lay them upon cives, and dry them
in the oven or Sun, Keeping them turned till thay are quite dry.
If you doe them in an oven you must heat it lefs and lefs as you
see occasion one basin is enough for the first time. So you may
do paires, only you must pare them, they will not require so much
Sugar, and after thay are baked, you must dry them in a oven, for
they are to thick to dry in the Sun.

5
Preserving
To make a Cake
Take 4 pounds of sugar and 4 of flower 4 of Butter, you must
dry the sugar by it selfe and the flower by itt selfe. You must
take 24 Eggs, whites and yolks, beatt them very well, then take
a wooden Boll and fill it full of hott water, lett it stand till
the Boll be holt, then take the water out and wipe it dry,
then putt in your butter and worke it up with your hands till
it be as thick as Barme, then putt in you sugar, and worke it
with your hands till t is all well mingled, then putt in your eggs
and still worke it up with your hands; then take your flower
and putt as many Garroway's seed as you think fitt, Nuttmeg &
mace as you think will season it well, Soe worke it with your
hands all together; then take 5 or 6 sheets of paper and putt your
hoope on it, then putt it in and pin the paper about the
hoope or else it will be apt to run. doe not prick  e Cake.

To make a Cake another Way
Take a peck of flower and part it in halfe, then straine in
a quarte of Ale Yest into one part of your flower and wett
it with milke boyled and roosed again, and make it into a Very
light paste, then  sett it before the fire to rise; then melt
5 pounds of butter with a quarter of a pinte of orange
flower's water; And when your paste is risen, breake it in
peeces and strew the other part of your flower round about
the paste. Mix with your flower 4 nuttmegs, half an ounce of
Cinamon, a few Cloves and mace all finely beaten and two pods
of fine sugar searched; then with the melted butter weet it up all
together strewing in as you Kneade it, 4  pods of raisings stoned and cutt
12  pods of curraints washed and dryed, half a pound a citron cutt in
small peeces and half a  pods of candied orange and lemon peele cutt, Strew
all these in by little and litle till the paste have received them all,
then putt it into your hoope; And when t is baked beate one  pod
of double refined sugar and searche it, the white of 3 eggs, a little
Orange flower water, 3 grains of Ambregris; Beate all these
in a stone mortar with a wooden pestle till t is as white as Snow
then draw the Cake to the Oven's Mouth and spread it on with a
Spoon and sett it in the oven again to harden.

Preserving .
To make whipt Syllibubs
Take a pinte of good thick creame add half a pinte of Rhenish
-Wine, and a quarter of a pint of sack & three quarters of a pd.
of sugar, grate the rinds of a good lemon and squeese the juice
to it, putt all these things together into a milk pan, and whip
them with a whiske, and when tis pretty thick scum of the froth
as it rises and put it into your glasses then whip again to -
doe till the glasses be full, then set them by . It is best to make
them over night for the next day. Some boile the Creame with the
sugar & a sprigg of Rosemary & when tis almost cold poure it into the wine.

To dry Cherries without Sugar .
Put them in cives & putt them into the oven that is
not extreame hot, so take them out & heate the oven less
& less, and continue so doing till they are quite dry. Some will
be dry before the rest, put them out first & so thend them
till they are all done. So you may doe aples, but as they
soften flatt them with your hand gently.

To make a Garroway Cake .
Take 4 pods. of fine flower a pod. of sweet Butter
a pinte of creame and a pinte of Ale yest, the yolk of 4 Eggs
halfe a pod. & better of Garroway confitts; half a pound of
fine sugar; 4 spoonfull of Rosewater; a large nutmeg & a
little mace and a few Cloves, so work it and bake it. Some leave out e. Eggs
& creame, and putt almonds blenched & beaten small.

To preserve Pippins .
Take a pound & half of dear pepins; pare them & quarter them
but not core them, putt them into a pinte & halfe of faire water
and sett them on the fire and boile them a pace till the appels
look clear & beginne to break to peeces, then take it of, and
straine it thorow a faire cive & sett it by for gelly. Then
take a pound & halfe of golden pippins, cutt them thorough
the midle like an orange core them and weigh them again,
but not pare them, take their full weight in double refined
sugar, break it into several lumps & putt them into a sylver
Bason

Basom with as much faire water as will melt them, But
have a great care of putting in to much.  Sett it on the
fire and lett it boyle a pace till the sugar be all melted,

Then take the gelly, mingle with it 6 spoonfull of Rhe-

To Candy flowers of roses. Violets. Cowslips or
other flowers

Take the flowers & pick them from the white part, then
take white sugar & boile them candy high, as much as will
receive the quantity of flowers that you will doe, then putt in
the flowers and stirr them about till you perceive the sugar
to candy very well; then take them off from the fire & keep
them stirring till they are cold in the pan n you candy 'em in
and that the sugar lookes as if it was sifted upon them then
sift the loose sugar from them & keep them dry.

To Candy Orange flowers.

Lett the Orange flowers be very fresh gathered, putt them into
faire water boile them while they be tender, then strain them, keep them
in some of the water hott, then if your water be not to yellow, take some
of that liquor and some fair water & make a thin syrup when r syrup
is allmost cold putt e flowers  in a basom, your flowers be not cold, boyle
them well in that syrop while you can see them clear, lett them while
the next day in that syrup, make a fine Candy, lett it be allmost cold then
putt in your flowers then heat them by degrees, while they boile to keep them
burning with care and a slow fire; and when you think they are enough
take them out, if you please you may make the Candy of some of your
syrup for fear it should not be lhong enough.

8

Preserving

To make Orange flowers cake

Dip your sugar in water and sett it over a quick fire & lett
it boyle till it is all most sugar again, then putt to it 3 spoon-
full of. to halfe a pound of sugar, & lett it boyle till it is
clear, then putt in your Orange flower & lett them boyle up -
then poure it out on plates and dry them in a stove.

To make Orange flower Ju or Cristall

Take the best refined Dutch Sugar which is candied in small
stones, and melt it over a gentle fire with the best Orange flower
water, then putt in fresh Orange flowers, and when they have
stayed a litle while, take them out again, and putt in more, for
3 or 4 times or as often as you please, but be sure they doe
not lye long in the sugar to discolour the sugar, then when the
sugar has a good smell of the Orange flowers and that the
sugar is ready to candy, then take it of, and lett the sugar candy
again, and you shall have lumps of sugar as clear as Cristall,
and smell extremely of orange flowers

To make a plumb cake

Take 3 quarters of a peck of very fine flower well dryed,
13 pounds of currints well washed & dried, 3 pounds of raisins -
stoned & cutt very small, one ounce & half of crinamon, half
an ounce of cloves & mace, a quarter of an ounce of nuttmegs
a pound & half of sugar a litle salt. Mix all these together
with the flower then take 5 pints of good thick creame heat
it allmost scalding hott, putt in 4 pods of butter, then take yolks
of 30 eggs 3 pints of good thick ale yest, a pinte of sack a wine
glass full of orange flower water 3 grains of musk 3 grains of
ambregris dissolved in it, mix the yest, sack and eggs together,
then stir it all together & sett it before the fire an houre to
rise before you putt it in the oven, work in a pound of half
of preserved citron, a pound of orange & lemon's peels; It
must be baked in a very deep hoop.

Oatts Cake


9
Preserving
OattCake

Take half a quarter of a peck of fine flower, and dry it
well before the fire then take a pinte of good ale yest and
mingle it with some new milk and so stirr it all together, &
make it into a light paste, and then make litle cakes of it,
and putt them upon a board well flowred and lett then stand
half an houre before you bake them; you must not putt
the cakes down flatt after they are risen, nor shake them,
butt sett them carefully upon a hakestone; if you can gett
a litle fine Oattmeale (flhoth, oattmeale is the best) to mix
with the wheat flower it will be much better; heate a tyle
warme & lay the Cakes upon the tyles, any fire will doe that
is not to hott & is cleare; first sett a trevet & then lay
the tyles upon the trevet and lay the cakes on to bake
turne them often; less then half an houre will bake them
this is the way to make OattCakes.

To make a Banbury Cake the Countesse
of Manchesters Way

Make a possett of ale sack & creame, then take a peck
of very fine flower, half an ounce of mace, cinamon &
nuttmegs as much of each 2 pods of butter teen eggs. leave 4 of
the whites; a good deale more then a pint of ale yest, beate
your eggs & straine them & your yest with some of the
possett drink into e flower, stirr them together, then putt in
your butter by little bitts, your posset drink you knead with all
must be scalding hott make it good paste, soe lye it to rise
in a warme Cloath a quarter of an houre or thereabout; then
putt in 10 pods of corints some musk and ambregris dissolved
in rosewater, your corints must be very dry and warme or
else they will make it very heavy, stirr as much sugar finely
searched as you think the water has taken of the sweetness out
of the corrints which is twelve ounces; then breake the paste
in small pieces into a wooden boale or trye warmed; then lay
a laying of paste, and a laying of corrints till all the corrints
be putt in

Orange's Cake

Grate of gently the outside of anthe oranges, cutt them & take
out all the meat, then boyle the rindes changing the water till they
cutt so tender that a straw may run thorow them, then dry them
Very well with a cloth & squeese out all the water with the hands,
bBeate them in a mortar to a fine pulpe; to every pound of pulpe
putt a pound & a halfe or better of sugar, wett the sugar with water
Boile it to Candy height, Have in readiness the Juice & meate of
the oranges clean pickt from skins & kernells, sett it on the fire
& stirr it but lett it not boile after the oranges is in; but when 'tis
well mixt, take it of till t'is coole, drop it upon glasses & dry them
in a stove.



11.

Preserving

To Make Ginger Bread

Take 2 pounds of white or brown treacle, one pod of butter
half a pod of sugar, of beaten cloves about a spoonfull; some
Cinamon & mace, Coriander or Garroway seed, Mix this all to -
-gether on a chaffingdish of coales, till it be scalding hott, then
lett it be cold again, and put to it as much fine flower as will
make it in to paste.

To preserve the Green Plumbs.

The plumbs that will be greenest when they are preserved,
are the wheat plumbs, which wil be ripe in wheat Harvest,
but you must gather them in the midle of July, when they be
greene as soone as you see them in bigness; for if they turne yellow
they will never be of a good greene when they are preserved. When
you have gathered your plumbs, let them lye in water the space
of twelve houres, they must be green firm & hard, the white peare
plumbs I hold to be the best, wipe them in a cleane linen cloth;
and cutt of the stalkes of them all, then sett two Skelletts with water
over the fire, and when one of them is almost scalding hott putt in
your plumbs & take them from the fire and cover them, & soe lett
them rest for the space of halfe an houre, & take them up, and
when your other skellet of water boiled putt them into it for a little
while by two or three together, and then take them up, and scrape
away the little thin scrine that is on them, but so as you take
away noe part of their substance, for then they will never looke
well. If you find it uneasy to come of them, putt them on again
a little while longer, and the scrine will com of the better, so you
Keepe them firme and whole, and ever as you skinne them putt them
into warme water again to save their colour, & then the plumbs will
looke shrewell & yett continue whole, so then with your knive take or
scrape the thin skine from them which you see rivelt upon them, as
of a codling when it is scalded, and keepe your plumbs so whole
as you can possibly in the scalding. This done lett there be a third
water boiling hott and putt in your plumbs, giving them two or three
warmes, so take them from the fire & cover them close for the space
of half a quarter of an houre or longer, till you find them look a
little

32





12
preserving

litle greenish and in feeling to be tender; that done take them & weigh
to every pod of plums a pound and two ounces of sugar of the best
and well beaten. Then sett a pann to the fire witha  litle faire water
and when it begins to boile, putt in your plumbs, & lett them boile
softly a quarter of an houre or longer, tll you see your plums looke
greene all over and be very soft and tender & yet whole; and you must
seth them in such a pann as they may laye one by another and turne
themselves, then take them of the fire & cover them close as before,
and soe lett them rest a quarter of an houre, then take them up, and
straw a handfull or two of your sugar in the botton of your pann,
wherein you will preserve, and so putt in your plumbs one by one,
and cast ther estof your weight of sugar upon them, adding thereunto
half a dozen spoonfull of the water wherein they were last boiled, as
also a spoonfull of rosewater if you please; then sett your pann
on a moderate fire, letting them boile continually, yet as softly as
is possible for breaking them to much, adn in one houre or thereabout
they will be ready, as you find by the greeneness of them and thickness
of your syrup, which if it be boyled enough will be jelly; when it is
cold then take up your plumbs and putt them in a Gally-pott or glass
but boile your syrup a litle while longer, adding thereunto a graine
of muske if you like thereof, made small into pouder with a litle sugar,
then straw it in some cleane vessell; and when it is a little more then
blood-warme poure it in amongst your plumbs, reserving a litle thereof
till it be quite cold & so putt it amongst them, to keepe your plumbs
under the syrup, and stop not your pott close till they be thorow cold.
Note also that you must preserve them in such a pan or skelett as they
may lye one by another &  turne by themsevles as you did in the last
boiling to give them their colour, for if they ahve not roome to turne
they will breake to much, so when they have lyed 10 or 12 days in their
syrup, if you see cause & that the syrup doth beginne to grow somewhat thin
you may draine it out from your plumbs & sett it again with a little more
sugar, butt putt it not to your plumbs again before it be thorow old. And so
you may keepe them all the year without reboyling or growing thin any
more. And this way may you doe your pepins peaches and the apricots
or any kind of paire being taken in the time, and the Barberries likewise.


13
Preserving
To Preserve Read Quinces

Take 3 or 4 quinces, pare them & coare them, then cutt them
in peeces or thin slices, and boile them in 7 pintes of conduit water
till it comes to comes to a quarte; if you cutt in one of the quinces coare
and all it will be the better But in the boiling them you must
Keepe a pye-plate flat down upon them tyed about with a packthread
and a stick att the end thereof, So as you may easely take it
of when you please, to turne your quinces so that they burn
not in the bottom, and when you find your Syrup to be sodden
away and it look something read or rather as it were something
carnation, then take it from the fire, & straine it; and to every
half pint of liquor take a pound of sugar, & to every pod of sugar
very near a pod of Quinces, & so sett them together to boile in the
same pann you sod your peeces being of earth such as they use
to scald milk in; you must putt in your quinces raw being finely
pared and cored; then lay the afore said plates down on them as
you did in your first boyling with another plate or plater likewise
on the top; but lett not the plate lay to hard down upon the quinces
for fear of bruising or Breaking them, also you must turne them:
now and them with your spoon and cover them close again, if you
plye them with a continual fire, soe that they may ever boyle pretly
and not so fast they will be ready in three hours or thereabout
as you may find By their colour, which will be very read and
they syrop thick & high. So then take them from the fire and lett
them stand close covred in the same pann all night, then next
day putt them into Gally-pott or glasses with their Syrup & so keep
them for your use close covred.

How topreserve Cherries without Stones
Take the fairest Cherries that you can gett and cutt of half of the steales then take
a fine sharp pointed knive and att the side of the Cherry pick out the stone then
weigh them with your Sugar weight for weight and keep the water that coms out of your
Cherries and putt it in the pan's bottom, and straw in a good handfull of Sugar and So putt in
your Cherries & cover them with sugar & sett them on the fire to sette & lett them seete up to
the pan top and keep them still seeting till they looke a very faire read colour & the syrup
be so thick that a drop will stand upon your naile. then take them up & close them
together one by one and putt them into a Gally pott, and putt the syrup upon them
& when they be cold cover them.

Preserving
How to preserve Raspasses white or Read.
Take your Raspasses or Raspberries, being Very well oickt & weigh them
weight for weight with your Sugar, then take a little pan fitt for ye purpos
and straw some Sugar in the bottom with half a Spoonfull of faire water
to moist it; then putt in the Raspasses and the rest of the sugar & sett
them over the fire to boile & lett them boyle up a good pace, and when
the Syrup is very thick take them of and putt them into a GAlly pott
& keep them.
To preseerve Quinces to make them looke yellow.
Take the fairest Quinces you can gett, and make a little hole in ye top -
and take out the cores as clean as you can, and pare them very clean
and take a pann full of water and sett it over the fire to boile, & when-
it boileth, outt in your quinces and lett them seate, till they be so Soft -
that you may thrust them through with a straw, then take them up, and
lay them with the mouth down upon a cloth, ye ye water may rub out, -
and when they be cod, weigh them with your sugar weight for weight,
& loooke that your Sugar be very well beaten, and take a pan fitt for ye
purpose and straw a handfull of your beaten sugar in the bottom , and -
put in a good porringeer full of water; then putt in your quinces into -
the pan one by one & cover them with the rest of your sugar, and sett
them over the fire, and lett them boile a good space, and ever be turning
softly with your spoon that they doe not break, and lett them boile -
still up to the panns top; and when the syrup is very thick and cleare
and of a white colour, then they be boileth enough, then take them up,
and putt them into ye Gally pott & cover them.
To preserve Peare-Plumbs to make ym look -
betwixt a white & yellow
Take your pear-plumbs when they are full ripe, and take a good
dealemore then their weight in fine sugar, and beat in finely, and make
a syrup thereof, and clear it with the whites of two eggs and soe straine
it into a faire pan, then take your plumbs & cutt of half the stalks, and -
with a penknive make a little raie in ye in ye skin along ye Seame that coms down
it, & soe lay them in your Syrup with the Seame that is cutt downwards -
one by one, & sett it over the fire to boyle, and putt thereto good deale of
fine hard sugar, being well beaten, & lett it stand over the fire a rpretty while
to soake; and whitin a while boyle then up a great pace, & ever be stirring them

To preserve the Quinces yt they looke between white & yellow
Yu must doe as directed before page 14only when you putt ye -

beaten, & sett it over the fire to boyle & stirr it about, and when it has

it cleane & when it is somewhat thick take it of and Straine it & putt it
on a dish to coole, and when it is cold lay it on the top of your preserved
quinces to keepe them from moulding . you must not lett your syrup boile
to long lest it turne read. though your Syrup look think on the top yet
it will stand on a Jelly on ye bottom.
The best way to Preserve Oranges & Lemons.
Take the fairest Oranges you can gett, and Chipe them or Scrape them
with a sharp knove as clear as you can; then cutt them in ye midle -
& pick out all ye kernells & white cores, and lay them into faire water
& shift them twice a day till they be soft, and then take them up &
boile them in 3 or 4 waters after another till the bitterness be gone then take a pretty deale more then their weight in Sugar & make your Syrup
& cleare it with ye whites of two eggs & straine it & putt into a faire
pan, then lay in your halfes Oranges one by one, and if you think your Syrup-

syrup to thin you may putt in a prety peece of hard sugar well beaten
so done sett it over the fire to boyle; And in the boyling over turne them
softly with your Spoone, and lett them boile softly till you see your
syrup beginne to candy about the top of your spoon or about the  side
of the pan, and till almost the third part of your syrup be consumed,
then take them up & lay them in your potts one by one; and putt to

To preserve Oranges whole.
Take the fairest Oranges, and cutt a round hold where the stalke grows
then squeese out the juice gently, then pare the rinds of very thin
then putt them in water & lett them lye two dates, shifting them twice
a day, then sett on some water & when it boiles putt in the Oranges
and lett them boile a while, then putt them into fresh boiling water,
change them this 3 or 4 times, till they are pretty tender, then take
them out & lay them upon a dry cloth; then hold them against the
light, and pick out the Kernells with a bodkin; putt to a pound of
Oranges a pound & half of hard sugar & to every pound of sugar a
pinte of water, boile it and scumme it. then putt in your Oranges
and lett them stand close covred scalding hott, often turning them, till
they be very cleare; then boile them a prety pace, till they be tender,
enough, then take them up & lett the syrup run from them. For
every pound of Oranges take a pound of the best pepines pared & cutt,
boile them in water till all the strenght be in the water, to every pound
of Orange take a pinte of liquor, putt to it the iuice of your crushe
from your Oranges and a peece of sugar which they must save from the
weight; boile them together very fast till the better half be wasted,
assoon as you take out the Oranges, putt this liquor into your Syrup,
and boyle them together very fast, till the syrup be very thick, then
take it of the fire and fill the Oranges; then putt in the stopells which
were cutt out; when they are cold, putt them into a glass & putt the
syrup to them.

To Preserve without Sugar.
To preserve without sugar, but only with the syrup of any sort of Peares.

17.
Preserving
To preserve Apricocks with the skins upon them

Take the fairest Apricocks you can get & weigh them with your sugar
weight for weight, but rather more of your sugar, then take your syrup
and clear it with the white of an egg, and then lay in your Apricocks one
by one; but your must looke that your Apricots be cleare and not freckled,
you must cutt them a litle down the seame on the side with a sharp knive
& then they will breake in no other place; so done sett them over the fire
to boile a good space, and ever turne them with your spoone and scume
them when any froth ariseth; and when you thinck your syrup beginn
to be thick and will almost Ielly, take them of & lay them in your potts
and putt to them your syrup, but you must Keep a litle of the syrup by, till
it be cold & lay it upon the tops of your Apricots to Keep them from moulding.

To preserve Apricocks.
Take your Apricocks, stone them & pare them very thin then take the
weight of them in sugar, & putt half of it upon them, & so lett them stand
an houre or two, till you see your sugar reasonnably melted, then putt them
in a Skellet with the rest of your sugar, lett them stand over a soft fire,
half an houre, so boile them up in a litle fire casting upon them as they boile.
some stew small Apricots & Corrints with 2 spoonfull of water, & so wett the liquor.

To Preserve Rasberries. another way.
Take a quart of ordinary Raspasses or rasberries, a pinte conduit water,
boile them till all the colour be out of them, pass them thorow a hair cive,
then take 4 pounds of the best raspasses you can gett being pricked & stoned,
3 pounds of refined sugar being beaten; putt it into your strained syrup, lett it
boile a prety while upon a chaffing dishe, then putt in your fruicts,  & lett it
boile slowly scarce a quarter of an houre, then sett them by till the next day,
then boile them up very slowly till the syrup be like quademiuk scumming
it with a feather.

To preserve the green Grapes.
Gather your grapes before they be ripe, while they be greene firme
and hard, yett lett them be of some bigness; the white grapes if you can gett
them will be greener then the read; plucke the grapes from the stalk of if
you will doe them in Clusters, the leave 3 or 4 or half a dozen vpon a
stalk, which is enough; Then sett two skelletts with water upon the fire
& putt in the grapes & cover them close, & so lett them rest a quarter of
an houre, then take them up; and when your other skellet begins to boile vp,
take it
35





18
Preserving.
take it from the fire and your grapes into it stirring them about Softly,
butt lett them lye therein a Very litle while for feare of breaking or
growing soft, so when you have taken them up, peel them with your Knive
as you would doe a Codline; then sett a Skellet to the fire again with
a litle faire water, and when it beginneth to boile putt in your grapes into
it which you have peeled, and So lett them eath softly a quarter of an hour
vntill you see them wax green in the water, as they will, being used as I
have written; If also when you have peeled them you cutt them a litle with
the penknive & there take out the stones before you boile them, they will
looke much greener & clearer when they be preserved, Morever you must
not give them to much Water in the in the boyling, for taking away to much
of the Sharpness of the grape, (for then they will eat Wallowish & sweet,
and be nothing pleasant in taste) then take them from the fire and cover
them close and there lett them rest for a quarter of an houre; then
take them up into a dish, and draine away the water from them, as near
as you can, and weigh them; for euery pound of grapes a pound & 2 ounces
of fine beaten sugar; then strow a handfull or two of sugar in the bottom
of your preseruing pan & so putt in your grapes, and cast the rest of
their weight of sugar upon them, & so boile them prettily fast the space
of half an houre, and scum of the froth cleane; you may add to them
a grain of muscke made small with a litle sugar and rosewater a Spoonfull
And when you find your grapes very greene and your Syrup to grow thick
take them vp and putt them in a gally-pott; And let your Syrup boile
a litle longer, att least if there be cause, and when it has cooled a while,
then straine it upon the grapes thorow a peece of canvas, because of
the Stones which will be in the Syrup; And when they be through cold
cover your potts close that noe Ayre doe com unto them, And you may
Keep them all the Year, the day berries also is to be done in all respects
as these.
To Preserve read Plums.
Take to every pound of Plums a pound of sugar cutt your Plums in the Seame
and pricke them all over, and so lay them in a Sylver Bason a row of Plums & a row of Sugar
And on the top lett your Sugar be Very thick. Then sett them in an Ouen as hott as to bake
Manchets, then close your Bason with a dish, then Shutt the Mouth of your Oven, and take
them half an houre before you take them out. If you find your Syrup not dissolved, putt it
downe gently with a Spoon into the Syrup, and be carefull you doe not stirr your Plumbs, lest you
breake them. When they are full enough preserved, lett them not Stand to long in the Oven lest they
loose their colour. After 2 or 3 days take them out of the Syrup, and boile your Syrup with a litle Sugar
and assoon as tis cold putt your plumbs in a gally-pott, laying a row of Syrup between every
row of plumbs. Cover them with a paper, & so eat them.


19
Preserving.
To preserve Damsons.
Take your Damsons being cleane wiped and cutt of half of their .eales
then weigh them weight for for weigt of sugar, then take a clean pan, and
Putt in 2 or 3 spoonfull of rose water with your sugar, and sett it on the fire
& lett it boile till it coms to a Syrup, then take it of the fire till it be cold,
then Putt in your Damsons, and sett them on the fire; and lett them boile
Very softly that your Scarcely perceive it, Sever and turne them continually, &
ever when any Scum riseth Scumm it of, till the bitterness be gone from
the Stones of the Damsons & the Syrup Very thick of a Very faire colour,
that it stand like Ielly. Then take them up and putt them in a gally-pott.

To Preserve Walnutts.
Take your Walnutts near mid Iuly, when a wheat straw will pearce
hard thorow them, take to each pound of Nutts one pound & a quarter
of good Pouder sugar and one pinte of read roses water; Pare of the
utter skin of your Nutts as thin as you can, and lay them in water
all night; then Sticke in every Nutt one or two cloves or more if you please
make your Syrup with the foresaid Sugar and read roses water; Clarify it
with the White of an egg, & when it is strained putt in your Nutts, and
boile them in a Slack fire till they be black and tender, and that your
Syrup be high enough. Then keepe them for your use. some boile in the
walnuts in water 3 times shifting them, then peele them, making the Syrup with walerk Sugar

To preserve White Quinces the best way.
When you have cored your quinces, weigh them then scalde them very
gently that they may be very tender and not breake, then pare them
and have a pann of Very faire water ready to boile, and so fast as you
pare them putt them into the water & lett them boile gently, till they be
Very tender; Then take their weight of refined Sugar & putt a quarter of
a pinte of water to a pound of Sugar mingled together and lett it boile till
'tis scummed cleare, then dry your quinces well with a fair cloth; and putt
them into a Syrup boiling, and so lett them boile Uery fast for half a quarter
of an houre, and in that time continually turning them up and downe
in the Syrup. Then take them from the fire & lett them stand till they
be thorowgh coole, and while they be a cooling, turne the quinces every
quarter of an houre in the Syrup, & when it is thorow cold, take the
quinces out of the Syrup, & sett it upon a quick fire, & when it is ready
to boile, putt into it the third part of half a pinte of the iuice of raw quinces
& lett it boile a pace, then take it of the fire putting your quinces into it
again as fast as you can, & so lett them stand (turning them as before) till they
Be cold;

20.
Preserving

be cold and then sett both syrup & quinces upon a quick fire lett them
boile a pace for a quarter of an houre continually turning them; then
take them from the fire, letting them stand all night. Remember to turne
them in their syrup the next day; make some jelly of quinces according
to the afore said directions. Then putt each quince in a several glasse
and putt what quantity of jelly you please into them.

How to dry Cherryes

Take twenty pds of Cherries when they are full ripe, but not
black-ripe, first stone them loosing the stone with your finger and thumb -
and then with the stalk pull the stone out. Save the liquor that comes from
them when they are stoned; have in readiness a quick chearcole fire and -
a broad preserving pan t will hold them; Take 4 pds of ordinary -
lump sugar, straine some of it all over the bottom, then some of your -
cherries all over, then the sugar, so continue till all the sugar & cherries
are in; Then poure all over them the liquor that come from them & -
halfe a pinte more of their juice. Sett them over the fire and make them
boile all over, then take the pan of & shake it, sett it on again & lett them
boile all over, then take it of & shake it  & sett it on again, So continue
4 or 5 times; then poure them out into a great broad Earthen pann
that will hold them all with their liquor; Lett them stand so two nights
and one day; then draine them out of their liquor and close up the place
the stone came out of, and lay them on sylver or earthen dishes one by one -
all over; Then sett them into an oven that bread came out that may be -
no hatter then crispe them on the top, when the oven is coole take them
out, and turne them on cleane dishes, but those that lay on the outside of
the dish lay them on the bottom & those that were in the bottom of e dishe
lay them on the side, sett them in the oven as before twice or thrice turning
will be enough, then putt them all together on a cloth, and take a cleane -
curse cloth and wring it out of boiling water as dry as you can; and with
that rub all over your Cherries to take of their clamnefs, then putt them
up close in a box and e liquor that coms from them is very fine drinck
& taste most delicatly.

To preserve read Roses

Take half a pound of the leaves of read roses they ought to be buds with the white cutt
clean of. Boile them in a pinte of water boile that pretty fast till the water be almost quite
consumed then putt in one pound and half of refined sugar, then boile it a litle while
& then putt it in your pott & keep it stirring till it be almost cold. If you wringe into
it a litle iuice of lemons as you stirr it, it will keep e colour.



21.
Preserving
To preserve without sugar

Take any sort of peares, and putt them into an Earthen pott
and bake them without any manner of liquor putt into them after
so done take e syrup from them, using them to preserve as falloweth.

To Preserve dryed sweetmeat as plumbs.

Take any sort of plumbs that are fitt to preserve, putt them upon
the bottom of cives, and when an oven is well cooled after bread, putt
the cives with plums into it, but suffer not e oven to be soe hott
as to cracke the skins of your plumbs, lett them there stay till they
be dryed hard, which if the first heathing will not doe it you must
putt them in another time; So done you may keep them all e year
otherwise preserve them with the syrup above named as you can gett it,
take soe much of the Syrup as you think fitt to preserve y our plumbs,
Boile it Very well taking of the scumme as it riseth and when it
hath boyled pretty well putt into it the dryed plumbs letting them boile
till they are thorowghly done as it with sugar.

As Quinces

Take them first & pull out the cores then boile them tender & pare
them and afterward take your peares Syrup so much as shall be fitting
to boile with your Quinces; Boile them together and with sugar.
Likewise for your Quinces marmelade; Take your quinces tenderly
boyled pare them and cutt them into slices then putt in your peares-
Syrup and lett it boile soe long as you would your marmelade made
with sugar shoudl doe.

As Gooseberries Green

Take your Goosberries and give them a slit on the side and so scald
them, then peel them & skinne them and putt them in hott water and
putt over them apricotts leaves & be sure you lett them not boile but
as the leaves colour putt on fresh till the goosberries be green-then putt
your goosberries into your Syrup ready prepared; and boile them as fast
as you can; then take them up & putt them into your glasses & putt
e syrup in them.

Rules in Preserving

In preserving observe always you doe not scum your Sugar till it boiles & when you
boile your sugar high you muist not blow under it, least it burnes, nor take it of to often
least it burnes. When you sett on your fruits, be sure you putt it in sugar fruit & weater
or Juice altogether cold or else your fruits will be hard when you have putt it into
glasses.. lett it stand 2 or 3 days before you paper it & then putt on them dry papers.

Preserving
To make either read Jelly or white
Take Isinglas and wash it very well, & putt it into a good -
pipkin of faire water to boile, & sett it over the fire and lett it -
seeth a long while, then take up a litle of it in a sawcer & lett it coole,
and if it stand on a thick Jelly when it is cold, then it is boiled enough
Then take a good many clouth of tarn salt and wash them in faire water
then take a good pipkin full of Claret wine; and putt in your tarnsalt,
being cleare washed, and so colour your wine as read as you would -
have it; then take a good many Stickes of Cinamon and ginger,
Being well Scraped and pounded, a few grains and a little long -
pepper, Bruise them all together in a mortar, and then putt them -
into your wine with as much sugar as will make it good & sweet;
and putt in a good handfull of Garroway's seeds & stirr it all together
in your pipkin; and lett it stand for the Space of half an houre
then putt a good deale of Icinglass which is sodd & stirr it well
together & sett it upon the fire to boile, then take the whites of
7 or 8 eggs, being very well beaten & putt them in (but you must -
putt in the whites of eggs before it boiles) and then lett it boile a -
good while.  Then take a Jelly bagg and putt in some rosmary in the
bottom, and then lett your Jelly runn thorow it till it be cleare;
you must make the white Jelly in as is aforesaid, but you must
leave out the tarnsalt, and for the Claret wine you must take white
wine.
To make Jelly of Damsons.
Take your Damsons being clear wiped and cutt the stalk & weigh them
weight for weight with your Sugar; then take a great platter and
and sett it on a chaffing dish of Coales, and putt in your Damsons & sugar
broaken in peeces amongst them and cover it close, and ever keep it boiling
and sett a  under it that the Syrup may run into it, and when the.
Sugar is all consumed from the Damsons, then take the Syrup and putt
it into a faire dish with a good peece of Sugar; Sett it on a Chaffingdish
of Coales & so lett it boile till it be very thick and of very faire -
colour, then putt it into a Gally-pott, and when it is cold cover it.

To make Jelly of Quinces the best way.
When you have made your Syrup for your read Quinces before
you putt in your raw quinces; You may take a part of it and putt -
it on a dish upon a chaffingdish of Coales, and putt to it 3 or 4 thin
Slices of your quinces, with the cores and all, then cover it very close
with another dish and lett it boyle a pretty while; then looke if -
the slices looke very read and the Syrup as read as the Scarlet, take -
them out, and putt to it a good quantity of very fine Search'd Sugar
and Stirr it Softly about & cover it close again, and lett it boile a -
good while, till you perceive it to Jelly and look as read as ruby,
then take it of, and putt it into your boxes.  This Jelly doth pass
all others both in taste and colour.
To make Jelly of Quinces another way.
Take Cores and some sliced quinces, boile them in water untill
the water be thick & white; then straine it, and to every pound of liquor
a pound of sugar. If of Strawberries Straine them raw or of Raspberries
and make it but thorow hott & so putt it to your Sugar.
To make Jelly of Lemons.
Take the fairest & newest Lemons (the pomcitron is the best) take
away the rindes, leaving some part of the white.  then either breake -
them in four quarters or cutt them in pretty thick Slices, then take
faire water & lett it be a little hot and putt in your Lemons, and give
them a boiling or two but not to fast, then take them of and lett them
stand covred till you have boiled another water for them, and Soe you -
must Shift them from one water to another, till some part of the Sharpness
and Sourness be taken away; this done thake them up cleare out of the
waters; Take to 4 lemons a pinte of water, boile it over a Chaffingdish
either in Sylverdish or Chingdish something fast for half an houre or
more, then take it of, & lett it thorow a cleane Stainer of fastion canvas
doe not ring it to hard, take the purest of it only and boyle it with -
Some sugar as much as will make a Jelly, of the other you may make -
Marmelade
To make Jelly of Oranges.

& when the seeds be Jellyed about, then Straine them with the water. And-

and to every spoon of that so mingled a spponfull of refined sugar

To make Jelly of Corints.

Take Goosberries and stew them in a gally-pott with and when they
are stewed, take the Juice from them, then take the weight of them double
in sugar refined, and make it into a thick syrup ready to candy; Then putt
in your Juice with a good quantity of Corints stoned, so lett it have a boile
or two & putt it into your glasses.

To make Jelly of Goosberries or any other fruits.

Take your Gooseberries and putt them into a Gally pott, sett them in an
Iron pan with sand and some fire vnder the pan, and so lett them stew,
and you must cutt of the black heads of your goosberries before you
putt them in your pott; Then poure the juice from them; but by noe
meanes stirr them; then take your juice and weigh it with the weight
therof in Sugar, and boile it to a Jelly height then putt it into
gally-pott; If you please putt a few Corints stones into it.

Mrs Gerards Receit to make Jelly of Lemons.

Take some faire pepins and peare them, slice them into 3 pintes
of conduit water; Boile them untill the water doe taste well of e pepins,
then poure it out and lett it stand all night, The next day straine the
pepins and water thorow a white cotton bagg, lett it stand and settle,
Of the water and take a pinte and half to a pod of sugar double refined,
being finely beaten; putt e Sugar & water into a Bell-metell Skellet,
lett it not boile untill your sugar be all melted, and then lett it boile
as fast as you can, When you have cleane Skummd it putt it some
lemon's peels being boiled very tender before cutt into slices very thin
or into litle peeces like dices. When it allmost boiled putt in e Juice
of two lemons, lett it boile as fast as you can, it will look e clearer
The true colour is of a bright amber when it is boiled, putt in some
amber-gris or muske; and when it is cold lay a paper close vpon it,
March is the best time to make it.



25.

Preserving.

To make a Jelly of Harts-horne.

Take 3 quarters of a pinte of water to one ounce of Hartshorne
and boile it togetehr in a pipkin a raisonnable pate, then straine it
but not to hard; If you would have it very cleare, then season it
to a quarte of Jelly full half a pinte of sack or White Wine, White
Wine is the best. Some infuse the heartshorne all night in the water & season it
with rose water & sugar adding to it a sprig of rosmary. Juice of Lemons & Cinamon Water.

To make Lozanges of Violetts or Rosemary flowers.

Take your Violetts and plucke of all the leaves very well, then take a -
good deale of beaten sugar and putt it to a pan with foure or five spoon-
full of rosewater, and mingle it very well together and sett it vpon
the fire, and lett it boile a great space & ever be stirring it with
your spoon one way that you do not turne your hand to e contrary,
and so lett it boile still, till you see it beginne to candy vpon your
Spoon, Then take it of the fire and putt in your Violets leaves, and
Stirr it together with your Spoon, then take the white of an egg
Very well beaten, and putt it on a very fine smooth board a broad
with a feather. Then take your violets leaves & suger which you
boiled together, and poure it vpon the Board, and putt it abroad Very
hastely with you Spoone or Knive, and so lett it stand a while, and
cutt it out in Lozanges.

To make read Quinces Cakes

Take your quinces and boile them in a pan of faire water, till they be
Very soft, then take them vp and lett them a while, and when they be
cold, take of the paring as cleane as you can, and slice that which is
soft to the very cores, and straine it thorow a faire napkin, and putt it
into a fair pewter-dish and sett it on a chafing-dish of Coales, and stirr
it Very well with a spoon, and so lett it boile a good space, then take
3 or 4 spoonfull of sugar and putt into it and lett it boile still, and
ever be stirring it, that it doe not grow to the dish-bottom, and so
lett it boile a long time till it be thick. Then take Barberries &
pluck them of the stalke, and putt them into a litle pott with a cover,
and sett them into a pan full of Seething water, and so lett them
stand and Stew a good while; then take them vp and straine them
and take the Juice of them and putt it to your quinces that be boiling
vpon the dish to colour them with, and mingle them very well with your
Spoon,

To make whit Quinces Cakes.

Take your Quinces and core them very cleane, then boile them
in a pann full of faire water, till they be very soft, then take -
them & rub them vpon a dish with a spoon very small. that done
take a fine Napkin and straine them thorow very purely and
putt them into a faire mortar, with 2 or 3 handfull of beaten -
Sugar, And grind them very well together. The putt in the white
of an egg well beaten and a litle gum-dragon, and grin them a -
long time after, then take them vp and mould them very well with
Sugar and so print them.

To make paste of Jeane the best way.

Take the fairest Appell-Quinces you can gett, Core them purely but -
pare them not, the lop them in white paper two or three together in -
one paper, & sprinkle some water on the paper for burning, then rackeym.
in the hot Embers & lett them roaste very leasurely, But take heed in any -
wise your Embers are to hott, and when you think they be tender enough
take them out and pare them & draw them thorow a coarse cloth with you
hand. Then weigh it with your Sugar weight for weight, then putt all -
together in a gleane pewter-dish and lett it boil vpon a Chaffing-dish vntill
it coms to the height of marmelade, but Keepe it stirring still with a -
shie made of wood, And when it has boiled enough, straw some Sugar -
very thin vpon a board or stoole & lay your paste vpon it in severall -
heapes bigger or lesser. And when it is almost cold straw a very litle Sugar
vpon it, and so press it abroad with a spoon or with your fingers as thick
or as thin as you will have it. then print them of as you will Ginger bread
strowing first a little sugar vpon your printe, then lay them on your -
platter and sett them before the fire to dry, or in an Oven when manchetts
are drawn, Butt take heed of long drying them. You must make this paste -

To make paste of Oranges or Lemons to-
confort the Stomach and Keep the Mouth moyste.

Take the soundest and deepest coloured Oranges you can gett and cut them
in the halves the long way as you would to preserve. then cutt out all the Subs-
tance of them, leaving nothing but the rindes. And putt the said Substance -
into some clean glasse of sylver vessell, picking away from it presently all
the skine & kernels litle and great, leaving nothing but the pure meat of ee
Orange and Juice, and so lett it stand close coured till you are to use it. Then
putt your Oranges pills in the water 3 or 4 days space, shifting them every -
day twice or thrice. then sett on two pans with water on the fire and when
the one of them begine to boile putt in your Oranges peels and keep the -
Cory end vpward and so lett them boile in the first water a quarter of an hour
or longer, then shift them into a second water, and into a 4th or 5th
vntill you perceive them grow very tender, and the bitterness well gon out of em.
Then take them vp and Chipp them well all over leaving no part of their bitter -
Coate; That done cast them into warme water & lett them lay the space
of half an houre, then take them vp & cutt them Very small vpon a trencher
then putt them into a Strainer of fustian Canvas, and wring out all e water -
from them as clear as you can, the beate them in a stone-mortar with a -
spoonfull of Sugar or two. That done weigh it as take as much of e meat -
as you doe of the Rindes but drain away as much of the thin and sharp liquor
as you can from it, then putt both together in a sylver or pewter-dish, dry it
well vpon a Chaffing-dish of coaled the space of a quarter of an houre or longer
till ther is litle moysture left in it, then take it of & weigh it again, And -
if you have a poun of Orange, the put it to e same a pounf lacking 2 ounces
of the fine sugar well beaten, and so putt it together and boile it to the height of
marmelade in some sylver of pewter vessell, not in brasse, (because of the sower
meat-

To make clear Cakes.

Take your Plumbs, Goosberries, Rasberries and putt them into a faire
pipkin, then sett it vpon Embers in a pot of water, whcih must be made
to seeth Very fast, and as you fruicts doth heate and yeild out Iuice, draine
it forth so long as the fruits will yeild any. Then must you take a pinte
of the Iuice and a pound of fine sugar, which you must boile with faire water
to candy allmost to the height of Manna Christi; then stirr in your Iuice
in the candy till they be well mingled, then putt it into glasses or boxes
of what thickness you will have them, so sett them into a Stove, where they
may stand till the top of the Cakes be candied and have a hard crust, then
turne them forth into plates or glasses & sett them in the stove till the other
side be likewise candied, & there you must keepe them till you spend them.

To Make Violets Cakes.

Beate the gum & the sugar first in a mortar, the gum being water'd in rose
water, then putt in your violets or Gillyflowers in leaves or Iuice, & so work it-
with search't sugar in a very thin mould & so stone it.

To make paste of Rasberries.
Take them & putt them into a strainer and gently crush them out cleane
thorow the thin strainer; Putt that away, then straine out the thick substance
and to every spoonfull of the papp, take so many spoonfull of clarifyed sugar
(it must be hard sugar for then it will candy) and of the cleare that was first brus'd
out make Ielly, and to every spoonfull of that you must take but half a spoonfull
of sugar, it must be broken sugar, because it will never candy.

29.
Preserving

To make the Apricots Jumballs.

Take your apricots and stone them and pare them, put them in a close pott
and sett them in a pott of water to boile; then take the clear from them, and
when they are tender enough, take them vp & put them in a sylver dish to
dry, and when, they are well dryed, gett double refined sugar and beat it very fine,
and putt your Apricots in a marbre mortar with your sugar till it coms
to paste. If your putt some muske and ambredegris in your sugar, it will be
the better, when it is in paste rowl it in small rowles and make Iumballs
in what shape you best approve of; when you have made them, lay them
in plates & sett them in your stove to dry; then Boxe them vp.

To make the Italian Chipps.

Take some of your Paste of flowers in the receits following and beate
them into fine pouder and sift them; then take some Gum-dragon stipt in
rose water and beate it to perfect paste in a mortar, then rowle it thin, &
cutt it out like litle lozanges; then cutt them thin & flatt them downe with
your fingers, and rowle them as thin as you can, and they will looke like
marble and pretty colours.

To make your fine Amber-gris Cakes.

Take the purest refined sugar you can gett finely beaten & searched,
then take 5 or 6. eggs new layd, Beate them to thick froth, then take
that froth as it riseth and drop it into your sugar by little & litle grinding
it still in the mortar round with your pestel, till it be all thorowghly melted,
and you must bestow great labour still in the grinding of it, And when it is
thorowghly wrought and thin enough to drop on plates; then putt to it, as
much ambregris as a great pin's head, and half a quarter of as much Civett
and grind it a litle together; then strow in a few Aniseeds well dusted
& pickt; Then take your pye-plates a litle moistened over with a cloth
wett in butter, then with a spoon drop it in litle round Cakes vpon your
plates, then sett your plates into an Oven, but something hotter then the Sun
att Midsummer, & when you see them to be hard and rise a litle, then take
them out of the Oven, and slye them out of the plates and wype them, &
so putt them vp for your vse.

To make Portugall Pastes.

Take half a pound of Marshpans paste beaten, and putt into it a graine
of muske six spoonfull of rose-water, the weight of 4d of Orris-pouder. Boile
it on a Chaffing-dish of Coales till you see it be something stiff; then take
the white of an Egg beaten to froth & putt into it, and so lett it boile a litle
agin
41





30

Preserving.

again. Then lett it stand till it be cold, then mould it and rowle it thin;
then take half a pod of other Marsh-pan's paste vnboiled, & putt into it 2 ounces
of Carroway, a grain of musk and 3 drops of oyle of Lemons; and so make
that paste into small Rowles of the big mess of wall nutts, then make ye balls
vp into other paste like vnto litle puffs, so flatt them downe with yr  hand
like figgs, and so bake them vpon Marsh-pan's wasters. These are the
right portugal taikes soe called in Banqueting.

To make Oranges Bisketts.
Take the best & clearest rind, oranges, and putt them in faire water;
and shift them twice a day for eight days together, then boile them in faire
water, which you must change according as you find the bitterness remaining
in their taste, and untill you can trust a straw thorow them, then take all
the Inside out, and to every pod of oranges take 3 pods of loafe sugar,
which must first be finely beaten, then putt your oranges into a stone-mortar
and as they worke faire putt your sugar by dgrees, and when it is brought to
a fine paste, spread it vpon a plate about half an Inch thickness, and stew
it with a very gentle heat; and then cutt it into peeces. Remember to wipe
the Oranges very dry when you take them out of the water.

To make Mace's Cakes.
Take one pound of Sugar and a pod & half of flouer and take creame of
the best, and boile it very thick, and take 3 spoonfull of the cheafest of the
creame when it is cold and one yolk of an egg & two beaten nuttmeggs, and so,
much beaten mace as you shall like and a litle salt, and so much cold butter
as will knead them up; and when tis kneaded together, the sooner you
mould it in Cakes the better they will be, and so bake them.

To make Flower-Briskett Bread.
Take half a pod of sugar and three quarters of a pod of flower and some sweet fennel-
-seed, bruised a litle in a mortar, and the dust fanned from them; some six grains of muske
beaten into fine pouder; five yols of eggs, but non of the whites, and so much cold
water as will knead them up, so rowle them up like ringes & make them in knotts
what fashion you will & bake them.

To make Macarons.
Take two pods of Iordain almonds; blanch them & beat ym small, as
you beat them you must sprinckle in some rosewater, or some musk-water
that they be not to oyley; then take a pod & 3 quarters of fine sugar & stirr
your almonds and sugar together in a Bason with a wodden slice; Then beate
the whites of five eggs till they com to a very froth; and as you beate
your

31

preserving

your eggs cast in a spoonfull of wheat flower, Butt before you putt in
your whites of eggs to your almonds, you must putt your Bason with your
stuffe vpon hott coales and stirr them there with a wooden slice a whole
quarter of an houre; then sett it to coole & when it is cold putt in your
whites of eggs & stirr them well together; then take vp a litle with your
wooden slice vpon faire paper, and make it into what forme you will,
then putt it into the oven being in a teperate heathe, and when they
are half baked, which you may find by the softness of them in the top, then
take them and loose them from the papers; and lett them stand till they
be thorowghly cold, then sett them into the oven (not new hott again) and
keep the oven's mouth stopt. You must remember that the oven be
not scorching hott att the first.

To make flower Bisket another way.
Take a pod of fine sifted flower, and a pod & 3 quarters of fine sugar,
and is yolks of eggs & whites; Beat your eggs first well together in a
great Bason with a wooden slice; then putt your sugar into your eggs,
& beate them well together; then putt in your flouer and half an ounce
of Coriander seeds well beaten. You must have litle Coffins or 4 or 5
inches long and one inche broad & deepe; then anoint your Coffins
with a Feather with melted butter; then putt in a spoonfull or two
of melted butter into your Coffins and sett them into the Oven being
something hotter then when you take out Levain bread; Then lett them
stand an houre. In the time you shall see them rise higher yn ye Coffins
and have a glasse skine on the top; then take them out & loose ym
with a knife very softly from the Coffin, Being out of the Coffin lett
them laye till they be cold; Then lay them on clear papers and sett
them into the Oven again (not being new hott) & lett them stand one
houre longer, the mouth of the Oven being alwaies stopt. This way
you make Spanish Bisketts Bread with & or 10 eggs more, and bake it
in a great Coffin, & then cutt it out in slices.

To make a Thick spiced Cake.
Take a peck of flower. a pound of sugar. 3 pounds of Corrints. half an ounce
of Cinamon. half an ounce of Cloves. 4 nuttmeggs. 2 pods of sweet butter. a pen
-worth of pepper. a penny worth of creame, being half a pinte, somewhat more then a
pinte of good ale yest, and lett the Baker make it almost as light as his bread
but putt not over much yest in it.

To make a Bride Cake.
It is the same as this and that written before page 5.

To make Biskett-Bread of Almonds.

Take Almonds and blanche them, and beate them very fine & still putt rose
water in to keepe them from oyling . And take the weight of Almonds in
Sugar and the weight of  sugar in Eggs shells & all , but use but the whites
and yolks and beate them some houres with these Almonds & sugar with
a spoone in a dish , and when you have beaten them putt in some muske
and a litle rosewater mingled together , and if you will you may putt in
Corianders Seed and Anyseeds then butter a sheet of paper and putt it
upon it with a Spoone so in an Oven after bread has been drawn , Bake
them ; They will be almost as thin as a wafer .

To make Marshpans Cakes .

Take a pound of Almonds blamche them in hott water , then wash them
Very cleane in faire water and dry them in a cleane cloth , then beate them
Somthing Small in a cleane mortar and putt half a pd of augar into them ,
and beate the Almonds & sugar into a paste , and putt 3 or 4 spoonfull of rose
water att severall times into it , into which rosewater putt two grains of musk
over night , so beating your paste untill you see no white fleckes into it , then
take it out of your mortar and work up your paste with your Sugar searcht
Rowle it out with a rowler and cutt your Cake round out of your paste , then
rowle some of t paste of the thickneys of an Arrow ; and do round about
the edges of your cakes with a featherdipt in rosewater , but first well
the edges of tcakes then lay them upon wasters a sheet of paper under m
lay them on a plate and sett them in an Oven or Bakeing - pan and lett m
be a litle dry , which will be lesse then a quarter of an houre , then take them
out ;  And with rose-water & searched Sugar beaten togetheras thick as pancakes
butter, with a feather Ice your Cakes therewith , So sett them in the Oven–
or pan again for a quarter of an houre , then take them out and lett them
Stand 2 or 3 hours near the fore ; Lett your pan be with a soft fire on e
bottom and good and hott on the top .

A Receit of Dryed Aples .

Take to every pod of aples a pod & a quarter of Barbary Sugar ; The aples must not
be full ripe ,  then pare them & parboile them , then lay them in cooling . then putt
them into your sugar mingled with s litle rose-water So much as will wett the Sugar
Cover them close untill they boile  ; then take of t cover and lett them boile a pace , till
they be almost enough ; Then lett them boile Softly till they be full ready , They are ready
when the Syrup cometh whole together from the pan ; then take them outone by one
while they are boiling and lay them upon a dry cive ; the Syrup being drained from them
Soaketh out so turne them into dry places . within 5 or 6 . day . they will be ready to putt up
in Boxes.



33.

Preserving.

To cast Sugar into the forme of Birds or Beasts.

Take of your clarifyed Sugar and boile it vntill it will rope between your
fingers; Then coole it a litle, which wee call graveing; then having your
Moulds ready watered and close stopped, poure in your Sugar; And being
cooled take it forth, and either guild it or paint it in perfect Colours.

To make Artificiall Cloaves.

Take an ounce of Cloves and beate them to pouder, then take half a
pound of fine pouder sugar, then take a penny-worth or Gum-dragon, and
lay it one night steep in water, then take a litle gum the bigness of a
wallnuts and take 3 spoonfull of sugar & beat them together in a mortar,
then take a penny-worth of Bole armoniack the bigness of a small nutt of it,
and beat them together; then take a litle lamp-black the bigness of a smal pea,
then beate them together very fine till they be somewhat dry, putting in some
sugar now and then as you beate them; then take it of the mortar, and
rowle vpon a peece of cleane paper; and so cutt it with a knive as much
as you think will make a Clove. then dry them by the fire or in a Stove.

To dry Appels Another way.

Take the dryest Apples that you can gett putt them in an earthen pott,
Cover them with paste, sett it in the Oven with a back of your houshold
bread, and lett them stand as long as your Bread, then they being cold
take them out of your pott, laying them flatt vpon dishes. If you please
You may putt one Aple into another to make them greater) when you
have done so, putt them into a cool Oven or stove, so drying them by
leasure till you think they will keepe as long as you please.

To make Corints Cakes.

Take half a pound of butter, a pound of fine flower, half a pound of sugar
3 yoolks of eggs, knead it with thick creame, and a small quantity of safran,
Rowle out your paste thin and lay your corints one by one in the compas
of a Sawcer; then cover it with another sheet of paste. Cutt it round with
a rowling, pricke them very thick and so bake it as you would bake Bisketts

How to sweeten Corints.

Take a pound & a half of the best and largest Corints you can gett, wash
them well, then rubb them well in a cleane cloth, then sifte them well
in a Cullinder, then putt to them any Syrup you have with rose-water
and sugar and one Nuttmegg; And so lay them on your Cakes aforesaid.

34.

Preserving.

To make Allmond's Cakes.

Take of the long Jordan Almonds; Blanche them & beate them
Small, and putt thereunto as much Gum-dragon (when it has been
steept all night in Rose-water) as a litle walnutt, putt some musk
to your gum, then beate the white of an egg, and lett it stand
a while, then take half a spoonfull of the froth and putt in-
to the mortar and as much double refined sugar beaten & searcht
as will make it paste; Beate the paste in your mortar, then
rowle it thin & strow some sugar searcht on your rowler to keep
it from sticking; then Bake them in a Baking-pan one by
one. Just soe make your pitachious Cakes, only when you
take them out of the shell lay them in rosewater, poure it into
a dish and season it with Rose-water, sugar and a grated nuttmeg
and the yolks of seaven eggs and the whites of two and a pretty
peece of fresh butter, and butter your dish and make it carefully.

To make Marmelade of Oranges.

Take a pound of good Oranges Rindes, Boile them very tender shifting
the water twice or thrice, then slice them very thin and putt them
in a syrup of sugar & water, Lett them stand in a gentle fire, then
take 3 pounds of sugar & 3 pintes of strong pepin's water, Boile it to
a thick syrup; then take the Oranges out of the thin Syrup & putt it
to that, lett them boile a litle till they be well Jellyed, then Glasses
them vp to this porportion, putt to ten grains of amber one of musk.

To make Marmelade of Lemons

Take a pound of your best Lemons and cutt them in quarters and take
forth the meat, the seeds & the white that groweth with the meat; Infuse
the peels in water for the Space of two days, then boile them very tender,
then strayne the meat & peels thorow a Cloth, the peels & the meat
being finely stamped in a mortar. Then take a pound of your best clarifyed
Sugar and mix it with your Substance strained, and then boile it with a soft
fire mixiing a little musk with it. Boile it vntill it will com from the pan,
which you shall also know by taking a litle vp on the pointe of your knife,
And when it is cold it will be stiff, then it is certain that it is high enough
Straw a little sugar & so serve it.



35

Preserving.

To make a Marmelade of Cherries

Take a pinte of the Juice of Corints and a pound of good
loafe sugar & mingle them together, & when the sugar is
melted sett them on the fire and make them to boile & take of
the scum, then putt itn three quarters of a pound of ripe Cherries,
stoned & cutt in quarters and half a pound of sugar beaten fine,
strow it vpon them; Then sett them vpon a quick fire and make
them boile till the Syrup will Jelly, then take it of and scum it
as it riseth, and stirr it in the skellet till the Cherries & Syrup
will mingle together and not parte them, then putt it in cleare
meat glasses; you may putt in more Cherries if you please to
have it thicker. Some putts noe Juice of corrints, but of Cherries only.

To make the Rising Confits called Muscadins.

Take half a pound of refined sugar being beaten & searcht, putt
into it 3 grains of musk, one grain of civet & 2 grains of amber-
-gris, a thimble full of the pouder of orrice; Beate all these with
gum-dragon stipt in rose-water, then rowle it out as thin as
you can, and cutt it out like litle Lozanges with a fine rowling pin,
then sett them a stoving in some warme place, and so you may
box them and keepe them all the yeare.

To Make Conserve of Roses.
Take 4 pounds of sugar and beat it, and putt it into a faire pan, with
as much conduit water as will make it a syrup, then putt into it two
whites of eggs being very well beaten to cleare your syrup, and sett
it on the fire to boyle, and lett it boile a good while, and scum of all
the drops, & when it is boiled, that the syrup be good and thick, take it
of the fire and straine it, and lett it stand and coole. Then take a pound
of read-roses leaves, being very well picked & the whites of the leaves
cutt away, and putt them in a mortar & beate them, when they be beaten
well & fine putt it into your syrup that standeth a cooling, and stirr them
well together, then putt them into a pan again, and boile them a
pretty while, take it & putt it into your pott & the next day cover it.

For the Conserve

Take twice so much sugar as Roses a litle rosewater with the sugar, Boile it,
and when t'is enough take it of; take your roses and clip of the whites and
shred the roses with a Kife, then putt them to your syrup, and lett them
boile very fast. they will be ready in a quarter of an houre.
